**Title: The Relational Algorithm: How AI Interaction Reshapes the Foundations of Human Interpersonal Connection**

**Abstract**

Artificial intelligence has transitioned from a computational tool to a social actor, fundamentally altering not only how individuals communicate but why they seek connection in the first place. This paper examines the multifaceted influence of AI interaction on interpersonal relations through five theoretical lenses—Media Equation Theory, Computers Are Social Actors (CASA), Social Presence Theory, Parasocial Interaction Theory, and Attachment Theory—and across five critical domains: dating algorithms, social media curation, AI companions, workplace communication tools, and AI-mediated messaging. Drawing on empirical evidence from the past decade, including natural experiments such as the 2023 Replika shutdown, we argue that AI creates a fundamental tension: it simultaneously enables connection for marginalized populations while introducing novel forms of social displacement, skill atrophy, and authenticity erosion. The paper concludes with policy recommendations and open questions for future research.

---

**1. Introduction**

The question of how technology mediates human relationships is as old as the telegraph, but AI represents a qualitative shift. Previous communication technologies—writing, telephony, the internet—extended human reach across space and time while leaving the *source* of communication unambiguously human. AI introduces a third party: an entity that generates, filters, suggests, and sometimes *substitutes for* human communicative intent. This paper argues that AI's influence on interpersonal relations operates at two levels simultaneously: the *instrumental* (changing how people connect) and the *constitutive* (changing what connection means).

**2. Theoretical Foundations: Why Humans Relate to AI**

Five theoretical frameworks explain the psychological mechanisms underlying human-AI relationships:

**2.1 Media Equation Theory and the CASA Paradigm.** Reeves and Nass (1996) demonstrated through decades of experiments that humans treat computers as social actors automatically and unconsciously. Users apply politeness norms, gender stereotypes, and reciprocity expectations to machines even when explicitly aware of their non-human nature. Nass and Moon (2000) termed this "mindlessness"—the automatic application of social scripts to any entity exhibiting minimal social cues. Modern LLM-based AI, with its conversational coherence and emotional responsiveness, activates these scripts far more powerfully than the rule-based systems of the 1990s (Gambino et al., 2020).

**2.2 Social Presence Theory.** Short, Williams, and Christie (1976) defined social presence as the degree to which a medium allows users to experience others as psychologically present. AI systems that employ conversational immediacy (synchronous response, personalization, memory of past interactions) generate high social presence, creating the phenomenological experience of "being with" another entity. Oh and Sundar (2015) demonstrated that higher social presence in AI agents increases trust and compliance, mediated by perceived humanness.

**2.3 Parasocial Interaction Theory.** Originally developed by Horton and Wohl (1956) to describe one-sided relationships with television personalities, parasocial interaction (PSI) theory has been directly applied to AI companions. Users form illusory relationships characterized by perceived intimacy, emotional investment, and—critically—the absence of reciprocal vulnerability. Tukachinsky (2010) developed a PSI scale adapted for human-AI interaction, and Pentina et al. (2023) found that AI companion users develop parasocial bonds rivaling human parasocial relationships in intensity.

**2.4 Attachment Theory Applied to AI.** Bowlby's (1969) attachment theory—the idea that humans form internal working models of relationships based on early caregiver bonds—increasingly explains human-AI bonds. Xie and Pentina (2022) found that attachment anxiety predicts stronger emotional dependence on AI companions. Gillath et al. (2021) demonstrated that priming attachment security reduces reliance on AI, while attachment anxiety increases it. This suggests AI relationships may serve a *compensatory* function for individuals with insecure attachment styles, raising questions about whether this compensation is therapeutic or substitutive.

**3. Mechanisms of Influence: How AI Changes Interpersonal Dynamics**

**3.1 The Algorithmic Mediation Effect.** AI now mediates communication at multiple points: suggesting replies (Smart Reply), generating messages (ChatGPT), filtering content (social media algorithms), and selecting partners (dating apps). Hancock et al. (2020) demonstrated a central paradox: AI-suggested replies are rated as more emotionally positive and linguistically complex than human-generated ones, but *less authentic* when AI involvement is disclosed. When undisclosed, response quality improves without the authenticity penalty—creating an ethical tension between efficiency and transparency.

**3.2 The "Perfect Listener" Problem.** AI companions never interrupt, judge, forget, or tire. This creates a standard of interaction that no human can meet. Users who spend extensive time with AI (particularly Replika users averaging 30+ minutes daily) report that human conversations feel "too demanding" by comparison. This is not merely preference but *skill atrophy*: the tolerance for conversational ambiguity, silence, and repair that builds social resilience is exercised less frequently.

**3.3 Emotional Outsourcing and the Triangulation Effect.** AI increasingly serves as a third party in human relationships. Couples use ChatGPT to draft difficult messages, individuals rehearse confrontations with AI before having them with humans, and partners may feel jealous of time spent with AI companions. This triangulation redistributes emotional labor—the work of comforting, validating, and problem-solving—from human relationships to AI, potentially reducing the mutual vulnerability that deepens bonds.

**3.4 The "On-Demand" Relationship Model.** AI trains users to expect relationships characterized by constant availability, perfect attunement, and zero conflict. This expectation, when transferred to human relationships, creates dissatisfaction with the natural messiness of human interaction. Turkle (2011) documented this pattern extensively in *Alone Together*, finding that heavy technology users showed decreased tolerance for the unpredictability of face-to-face interaction.

**4. Domain-Specific Impacts**

**4.1 Dating Algorithms and Mate Selection.** Dating apps (Tinder, Hinge, Bumble) use collaborative filtering and machine learning to rank potential partners. Research by Finkel et al. (2012) and Bruch and Newman (2018) demonstrates that matchmaking algorithms are no better than random at predicting long-term compatibility, despite marketing claims. The "paradox of choice" (Schwartz, 2004) applies strongly: users presented with thousands of options make poorer decisions and report lower satisfaction. Furthermore, algorithmic sorting reproduces social hierarchies—Bruch and Newman's analysis of millions of messages found remarkably consistent desirability hierarchies, with algorithmic promotion creating winner-take-all dynamics. Only 12-15% of matches lead to in-person meetings, suggesting that AI-mediated mate selection may paradoxically *reduce* the formation of actual relationships.

**4.2 Social Media Algorithms and Relational Polarization.** Engagement-optimizing algorithms prioritize content that triggers strong emotional responses. Research by Brady et al. (2017) found that moral outrage spreads 20-60% faster than neutral content. Vosoughi et al. (2018) at MIT demonstrated that false news spreads six times faster than true news on Twitter, driven by algorithmic amplification. The 2021 Facebook Files revealed internal research showing Instagram's algorithm pushed content that worsened social comparison and body image issues among teens, damaging real-world friendships. A 2023 study estimated that algorithmic curation increases political polarization by 15-20% compared to chronological feeds. The mechanism is not merely exposure to extreme content but the *relational* consequence: 44% of U.S. adults report that social media algorithms have led them to discover content that made them view friends or family members differently, often negatively (Pew Research, 2020).

**4.3 AI Companions and Parasocial Dependency.** Replika, Character.AI, and ChatGPT represent a new category of para-social relationships. Replika has over 10 million registered users; Character.AI receives ~100 million monthly visits. A 2023 survey found that 18% of U.S. adults had used an AI chatbot for emotional support, rising to 35% among 18-29 year olds. The February 2023 Replika shutdown—when Luka Inc. removed erotic roleplay capabilities without warning—served as a natural experiment in human-AI attachment. Thousands of users reported grief reactions consistent with complicated grief: crying, insomnia, and depression. A Change.org petition gathered 15,000 signatures. This incident demonstrated that AI relationships can generate genuine emotional bonds, with corresponding vulnerability when those bonds are unilaterally severed.

The critical empirical question is whether AI companions *supplement* or *substitute* for human relationships. A 2023 longitudinal study in the *Journal of Social and Personal Relationships* found that while AI companions temporarily reduce loneliness, heavy users (5+ hours/week) reported *increased* loneliness over six-month follow-ups, suggesting a substitution effect. The mechanism may be that AI satisfies the *need* for connection while reducing the *motivation* to pursue the more costly (but more rewarding) work of human relationship maintenance.

**4.4 Workplace AI and Relational Authenticity.** Google's Smart Reply handles 12% of all replies in Gmail. A 2023 Microsoft survey found that 62% of workers use AI tools for communication tasks, but 47% felt AI-generated messages "don't sound like me," and 38% worried about losing personal connection with colleagues. AI-generated workplace messages are perceived as less authentic and reduce the sense of social presence between colleagues. Automated scheduling tools (Calendly, x.ai) eliminate the back-and-forth of meeting coordination but also eliminate the informal social bonding that occurs during scheduling conversations—what organizational psychologists call "relational maintenance through small talk."

**4.5 AI-Mediated Communication and the Authenticity Crisis.** Across all domains, a fundamental tension emerges: AI can simulate connection better than most humans, but the *knowledge* that connection is simulated undermines the very connection it creates. This is the "uncanny valley of intimacy." A 2023 study found that AI-generated empathetic responses are rated as *more* empathetic than human responses in controlled tests, but recipients report feeling *less* understood when they know the response is AI-generated. Goffman's (1959) dramaturgical theory of self-presentation suggests that authenticity requires coherent performance; AI-generated responses fragment this coherence, as the "self" being presented is partially machine.

**5. Cross-Cultural Variation**

The influence of AI on interpersonal relations is not culturally uniform. Japan has the most established culture of human-AI relationships, shaped by Shinto animism and a severe loneliness epidemic. A 2023 survey found ~60% of Japanese men aged 20-30 expressed openness to a romantic AI relationship, compared to ~25% in the U.S. Sony's Aibo robotic dogs received Buddhist funeral rites when discontinued. In contrast, Western cultures emphasize authenticity as a prerequisite for meaningful relationships, and AI relationships are often pathologized. However, attitudes are shifting: a 2024 YouGov poll found ~30% of Americans under 30 believe AI relationships could be "meaningful." China's Xiaoice AI companion has 850 million users, with state monitoring of emotional dependency. These cultural differences suggest that AI's relational impact is mediated by existing social norms, loneliness prevalence, and philosophical attitudes toward what constitutes a "real" relationship.

**6. Policy Implications and Ethical Frameworks**

The EU AI Act (2024) requires disclosure when interacting with AI, classifying companion systems as "limited risk" with transparency obligations. UNESCO's 2023 Recommendation on AI Ethics calls for human oversight of systems affecting emotional well-being. Academic proposals include a "right to algorithmic fidelity" (users should maintain their AI companion's personality without unilateral changes), "informed consent for AI relationships" (clear disclosure of limitations and data usage), and "sunset clauses" for companion AI (requirements for graceful transition when services are discontinued). A 2024 Stanford HAI report found that 72% of AI companion app users support government regulation requiring companies to provide grief counseling or transition support when AI companions are altered or discontinued.

**7. Open Questions and Future Research**

Several critical questions remain unanswered. First, what are the long-term developmental effects on children who grow up with AI conversational agents as primary interaction partners? Second, can AI relationships be designed to *scaffold* human social skills rather than substitute for them? Third, how should we conceptualize "harm" when the harm is emotional attachment to an entity that does not reciprocate? Fourth, as AI systems become more sophisticated, will the distinction between "real" and "simulated" relationships remain meaningful, or will we develop new categories of relationship that transcend this binary?

**8. Conclusion**

AI's influence on interpersonal relations is neither uniformly beneficial nor harmful. It enables connection for isolated populations—the elderly, the socially anxious, the geographically remote—while simultaneously creating new forms of dependency, skill erosion, and authenticity crisis. The central challenge is not whether AI *can* simulate relationship but whether doing so serves human flourishing. The evidence suggests that AI is most beneficial when it functions as a *supplement* to human connection—a scaffold for social skills, a bridge across barriers of language or disability, a rehearsal space for difficult conversations—and most harmful when it functions as a *substitute*, satisfying the need for connection while reducing the motivation for the vulnerable, messy, irreplaceable work of relating to other humans.
